The file itself may be gone when the entry is opened (it is swept when no saved request names it), and the send says so. (8) Cap 500 (Settings, 1 to 5000), enforced in the transaction that writes an entry; a setting keeps no response bodies; a body is masked first and cut to 1 MB after. Search is in memory over the loaded entries, by words: each must be in the URL, or be the method or the status. The list reads whole entries, bodies included; a second store for bodies is the step to take if that shows. (9) Opening an entry loads its template (scripts and tests included, where it kept the composer's own before) and shows the recorded response. **Guards (P2.5):** (10) A request is resolved twice only when it uses a secret: the first pass lists the ids, the vault is asked (the shell opens the unlock dialog on `SecretsVault.unlockRequested`; closing it sends nothing, D4), and the second pass has the values. A request without a secret is still built in the task of the click. All of it runs inside `buildRequest`, so after the pre-request script once scripts run again. (11) D3: a request with a `{{variable}}` without a value throws `UnresolvedVariablesError`; the composer shows which and offers "Send anyway"; a setting switches the guard off. (12) The wire check (C-007) is unchanged apart from its message, and "Send anyway" does not pass it. With no vault at all, a request that names a secret gets the dialog that makes one, and is then refused because the vault does not have that secret. **Exports:** (13) Export → Copy as cURL and Copy as HAR mask by default and have a "with credentials" twin each; the composer's Copy as cURL is a menu of two and never reads the vault (a reference stays as written); a collection's Export masks and "Export with credentials" does not. A vault secret is masked in every one of them. (14) **Environment export is not changed here.** P2.8 lists it, but a protected value is a reference, not a plaintext, and P2.11 gives environment export its three choices; masking plain variables by name now would be undone there. **Claims:** C-007, C-008 and C-014 reworded; C-045 (unresolved variables) new; 44. Limitation bullets for #60, #71 and #72 removed. **Tests whose claim changed:** the C-007 tripwire (dialog, cancel, then the missing secret); C-008 and C-014 (masked, and the opt-in); C-020 and the two C-035 e2e (menu item by exact name); the composer's "leaves an unresolvable {{var}}…" (held back first, then sent as written); "loads a history entry as what was sent, keeping the scripts and tests being composed" is now "…as the request it was composed as"; history fixtures across nine specs use `historyEntry()`; `findByUrl` went with its index and its test became the cap test. **Found on the way, in the tests themselves:** the scan's base64 reader first cut a run to a multiple of four characters and so could not see a secret at the end of one; fixed in the unit oracle and the e2e scanner before either was relied on, and each now has a test that it finds a secret at every offset. **Known and left:** a history entry's `template` also carries the `id`, `meta`, `name` and place of the saved request it was opened from, because `WorkspaceStore.snapshot()` returns them (it did before this PR). Closes #60 (F03), #71 (F14), #72 (F15), #64 (F07). **P2.11:** as specified, with these decisions. (1) **A restored collection is untrusted.** The AC asked for stores deep-equal after backup, reset and restore, except `meta.updatedAt`. A backup is a file, and D6 says a file's scripts are untrusted until approved; a file that could say "trusted" about itself would be the supply-chain vector F41 names. Asked, the maintainer chose "mark untrusted". The AC as built: deep-equal except each collection's `scriptTrust`; `meta.updatedAt` is equal as well, since records are written back as they are. The AC text in section 9 carries the note. (2) The file is `wayfarer/workspace/2`: `{$id, exportedAt, stores}`, where `stores` holds `collections`, `folders`, `requests`, `environments`, `secrets` and `meta` as stored, and `history` when asked for. **The files of request bodies are not in it** ("full" in the task): a file may be 50 MB and an import is capped at 10 MB; a request names its file, as in a collection file, and says so when sent without it. Restore removes the stored body files. (3) Restore checks every record with the validators the single-store importers have (`validateCollection` per collection with its folders and requests, `validateEnvironmentExport`, `validateVaultFile`, `validateRows`, `validateRequestContent` for history), refuses the whole file with the reasons, and otherwise replaces the stores in one transaction and reloads the page. History is replaced only when the file has one. (4) `navigator.storage.persist()` is asked when a request or an environment is saved, and again at the next save when the browser said no. Settings shows the answer and `storage.estimate()`. (5) The reminder counts from the last backup, or from the first use when there is none, and is worked out when the page loads. "Not now" puts it off 14 days. (6) The Safari notice is shown in Settings when `navigator.vendor` is Apple's and the page is not installed (`display-mode: standalone`). (7) Environment export opens a dialog with the three choices. "With the vault" puts a vault file under the `vault` key of the environments file, and Secrets, "Import vault" reads it from there. "Plain text" needs the vault unlocked and `EXPORT SECRETS` typed. The Export button in Settings writes the default (nothing of protected variables). (8) **Claims:** C-046 (backup and restore) and C-047 (environment export) are new; 46. The limitation bullets for #73 and #74 are reworded to what remains true: a browser can still delete the data. Closes #73 (F16), #74 (F17). **Exit criteria, 2C: met.** Vault secrets reach the wire and are found nowhere in storage or exports (the scan test is green in 3 engines); the vault can be rotated, exported and locks itself; history is capped, searchable and redacted. **Budgets** reset in the P2.11 PR to the measurement after 2C (Appendix A). **Open at the end of 2C:** PR #181 (claim C-042), left alone as instructed; no finding is open from this session. |

| 2026-10-10 | v1.2.5 | **Session 3A as executed**, one entry per PR. **Maintainer answers** (the prompt's three answers were left as placeholders; asked before any change): Q5, merge on green CI; D24 still holds; the two QuickJS packages are approved. **P3.0:** Phase 3 re-read against `main` at `c2bec7c` and split into 3A (P3.1, P3.8, P3.2, P3.7, P3.6) and 3B (P3.5, P3.3, P3.4, P3.9, P3.12, P3.10, P3.11, P3.13). The split is the one the session prompt suggested; the order inside each session is new, and the reasons are in section 9 under the delivery table. What the re-read corrected: (1) **F65** (#213): the executor drops a script's `logs` and `error`; P3.1 and P3.2 fix the halves. (2) C-006 changes in P3.1, which turns scripts on and deletes the banner; P3.11 keeps C-040 only. (3) The engine is in core and is handed the QuickJS module, so core's tests and the CLI run it in Node; the dependency is `quickjs-emscripten-core` plus the one variant, not the umbrella package that also depends on the debug and asyncify builds. The maintainer approved `quickjs-emscripten`; the core package is the part of it that is used. (4) The VM's global grows by task: `require` with P3.5, `postman` and the legacy globals with P3.4. (5) `scriptTrust.hash` arrives with P3.8, with no change of `DB_VERSION`: a missing hash is untrusted. (6) `pm.sendRequest` goes through `TransportRouter` (`RoutePolicy` is P6.4); `pm.cookies` is unsupported until P5.3; the runner-only calls have no effect until P5.2. (7) The redactor's path in section 4.2. (8) The release is task P3.13. (9) Two rules added from 2D's findings: a limit test asserts the message and not the time; the service worker is not changed to cache the `.wasm` file, since it already caches hashed same-origin files on first use (F64 stays as it is). Left for 3B to decide before it starts: whether P3.10's wall-clock gate can hold on a shared CI runner, and whether `newman` can enter the lockfile with `npm audit` at 0. No code changed. **P3.1:** as specified, with these decisions. (1) **Three files.** `packages/core/src/scripting/host.ts` (`runScript`: one runtime per run, the limits, the host functions), `vm-bootstrap.ts` beside it (what a script sees, as JavaScript text evaluated inside the VM before the script: `pm`, the small `expect`, `console`, `atob`, `btoa`, `setTimeout`), and the app's `quickjs.worker.ts`, which loads the engine once and passes messages. `ScriptSandbox` keeps its name and signature. (2) **The host object is never on the VM's global.** The bootstrap is a function called with it; a script reaches a host function only through the five names. Host functions take and return strings, numbers and booleans and check each argument's type, since a script can replace `String` under the bootstrap: a unit test does that and gets `Expected a string.`. So the rule P3.2 states is already how the boundary is built; P3.2 adds the caps, the console in the Tests tab and the redactor. (3) **The stack limit is 256 KB, not the plan's 1 MB, and it is not what stops a deep recursion in two engines of three.** Measured in a worker of the built app with a recursion 100,000 deep at limits from 128 KB to 4 MB and with none: QuickJS throws its own catchable "stack overflow" only when its count ends before the browser's stack under the WebAssembly does; that is at 128 KB in Chromium, at 256 KB in Firefox, and at no size tried in WebKit. Above that the browser throws from inside the engine call (`RangeError` in Chromium and WebKit, `InternalError: too much recursion` in Firefox). At 1 MB every engine did the second. `runScript` reports both as the stack limit, with one message, and after the second does not dispose the runtime (it would abort); `ScriptSandbox` ends the worker after any limit, so the next run has a new engine. A script may recurse about 1,500 calls deep in Firefox and between 740 and 1,500 in Chromium; `docs/scripts.md` says so. The AC of P3.6 (a recursion 100,000 deep gives a stack error) holds in all three. (4) **Timers.** A timer's callback stays in the VM; the host is told a number and a delay and asks for that number to be run. After the script, promise reactions and timers run until none is left or the deadline passes; a timer due after the deadline is not waited for. No `clearTimeout`, no `setInterval`: P3.1 names `setTimeout` only. (5) **The worker is kept between runs**, each run in a new runtime; a limit, a worker error or a failed load ends it. The page arms its own timer when the worker says the engine is loaded, one second past the script's deadline, so a cold download is not counted against the script. Part of P3.6 is therefore done here; P3.6 keeps the tests in three engines. (6) **Loading.** The worker imports the engine's `.wasm` file by its path under `node_modules`, and `".wasm": "file"` in `angular.json`'s `loader` gives `media/emscripten-module-<hash>.wasm` (503,134 B, 231,526 B gzip). The worker assembles the variant from the package's `./ffi` and `./emscripten-module` entry points with static imports: the worker is one lazy file of 26,796 B plus a 34,746 B chunk of `quickjs-emscripten-core`. The service worker is unchanged: its cache-first rule for hashed same-origin files takes the worker, the chunk and the `.wasm` file on first use, and `e2e/service-worker.spec.ts` runs a script with the server stopped. Initial bundle 1,209,737 B raw and 306,297 B gzip (1,209,797 and 306,352 before): budgets unchanged. (7) **CSP:** one keyword. C-011's test now asserts `script-src 'self' 'wasm-unsafe-eval'` exactly, in the header, the `<meta>` and on a script file. No violation event in any engine; nothing else was needed (R15 did not fire). (8) **F65, first half:** a script that ends in an error adds a failed row, "Pre-request script" or "Post-response script", after the tests that ran. The test was shown failing against the old behaviour. The request is still sent after a failed pre-request script, as before; Postman stops there, and P3.9 (3B) decides. (9) **`envMutations` is still a record**, built with `Object.fromEntries` from a `Map` and read with `Object.entries`; a variable named `__proto__` is an own key (unit test). An empty value still removes the variable. P3.3 replaces it with the scopes. (10) **Tests kept:** all ten of `script-sandbox.spec.ts` pass unchanged against QuickJS. The executor's "with scripts disabled never calls the sandbox" test had the deleted token as its subject; it is now "runs both scripts in every build" (C-006), and P3.8 brings back a skip for untrusted collections with its own test. The F01 tripwire asserts the script ran. (11) **Found in review, before merge: by the package's `./wasm` entry the import worked in the production build and failed under `ng serve`**, so scripts would have stopped running in local development. The development and unit-test servers hand an import that names a package to Vite, which tried to run the `.wasm` file as a WebAssembly module. Imported by path the file is built with the worker in all three. The e2e suite runs against the production build, so it could not show this; the check was one script test run against `ng serve`. **Two additions to `vitest.config.mts`, for the unit-test server only:** it puts the worker under the spec's directory, where the engine file is not (a middleware serves it from the package), and Vitest's browser mode rewrites `import()` to a helper that exists on the page and not in a worker (the worker files it serves get that helper; `quickjs-emscripten-core` imports its own module on demand). The built app needs neither. (12) **Licences:** the three packages are direct dependencies and are in `/3rdpartylicenses.txt` with QuickJS's own notice (Fabrice Bellard, MIT); nothing was added to `build-sw.mjs`. (13) C-006 reworded in the ledger, README and Trust Center; `docs/scripts.md` rewritten for QuickJS (P3.7 adds the test references). (14) **Found by CI on the PR:** the unit test of the memory limit filled 64 MB a megabyte at a time, which took over 5 s on the runner, so the script was stopped by its time limit first. The test now allocates 8 MB at a time with the deadline far away; the row is in `e2e/FLAKES.md`. Closes #58 (F01). **P3.8:** as specified in intent, with one change of form and these decisions. (1) **One digest per script, not one hash of the collection.** D6 and the task said "a SHA-256 hash of all scripts in the collection". With one hash, deleting or moving a request changes it, so either the user re-reviews a collection after deleting a request from it, or every write to the `requests` store re-hashes, which approves whatever was written, an importer's writes included. Neither is what D6 wants. With `approved: string[]`, a script is approved by its own text: removing a request needs nothing, and the only writes that add a digest are the review and a save from the composer. It is also stricter: a script changed by any other road has a digest the list does not hold. SHA-256 comes from WebCrypto, which is asynchronous, so digests are computed outside the IndexedDB transaction (one inside would end it) and written by `approveScripts` in a transaction of its own. If that write is lost the script stays unapproved: it fails closed. (2) **What is checked is the stored request the composer was loaded from, never the draft**: what the user types after loading is their own. The check runs when Send is pressed (`ScriptTrust.check`), from what is stored then; the banner reads a signal that follows it. A request with no stored script is still sent in the same task as the click. (3) **Saving does not launder.** The composer passes "were my scripts allowed to run" into Save and Save As; only then are the saved scripts' digests added, and only to a collection that is trusted. A script from an untrusted collection saved into a trusted one stays unapproved there and shows the banner; the unit test does exactly that. (4) **History.** A history entry carries the scripts of the request as sent, and opening one binds the composer to nothing, which would have counted as "written here". Its scripts wait for a review of their own (the same dialog, listing the entry's two scripts); the approval lasts while that entry is in the composer and stores nothing. An entry without scripts is not held. (5) **The review dialog** is one read-only Monaco editor holding every script under a comment line that names its request, not one editor per script: a collection of fifty requests would mount a hundred editors. (6) **The list only grows on edits** (64 characters per saved version of a script); a review starts it again. Marked in the code with its ceiling. (7) **Not done: collection- and folder-level scripts** do not exist until P4.9; when they arrive their digests join the same list. (8) The e2e seed helper writes the digests of the scripts it seeds, as a collection made here has them. (9) Screenshots of the banner, the Tests tab note and the dialog were read at 390 and 1440 px in both themes; the first showed the banner's icon alone on a row at 390 px, fixed. (10) Claim C-051; the limitation bullets for #98 are gone. The trust tests were shown failing with the check answering "yes" as before (5 of 8). Closes #98 (F41). **P3.2:** as specified, with these decisions. (1) **The boundary was built in P3.1** (strings, numbers and booleans, each argument's type checked by the host). P3.2 adds the AC's test: a script hands over an object with a getter, a prototype and a function, as a console argument, a variable's value and name, a test's name and a thrown value; the result is equal to its own JSON round trip and every value in it is a string. The getter is read once, inside the engine. (2) **Console caps:** 1,000 lines and 1 MB (characters); the line that passes the cap is cut to what fits, then one line `[console output truncated]`, and the script goes on. (3) **A cap the plan did not have:** test results and variables are kept by the host, outside the engine's 64 MB, so a loop of `pm.test` calls would have filled the worker's memory instead. They share 1 MB; the write that passes it throws into the script, and so does every write after it. (4) **The console is shown** in the Tests tab under "Console", the pre-request script's lines first (F65, second half). (5) **Masking.** Console lines, test names and messages and a script's own error go through `Redactor` with the request's vault secrets and credentials before they leave the executor: nothing unmasked reaches a signal. **A variable a post-response script sets is masked for vault secrets only.** Masking credentials there too would turn `pm.environment.set("token", pm.environment.get("token"))` into storing `***` over the user's own token, which is in the environment already; the invariant to keep is that a vault secret's plaintext is stored nowhere but the vault. A pre-request script's variables are stored as written: it runs before any secret is resolved and was given a secret only as its reference. P3.12 (3B) makes setting a protected variable write to the vault. (6) **The scan** (`e2e/secrets-wire.spec.ts`, C-007) gained a post-response script that reads the echoed secret and writes it to the console (twice), a variable, a test's name, a test's message and its own error; the scan reads the environments store and the Tests tab's text with everything else and finds 0 in three engines, and asserts the mask is there in its place. (7) Failure first: with the console dropped as before, `expected [] to deeply equal [ 'before', '[warn] careful', 'after' ]`. Closes #213 (F65). **P3.7:** as specified, with these decisions. (1) **Three tests in three engines** (`e2e/sandbox-escape.spec.ts`, claim C-052), each a script whose `pm.test` rows are the attempts; the page requires every row to pass and then looks from outside. (2) **Roads, not names:** 35 host names are looked up by eight roads to the global object (plain `typeof` through `Function`, indirect `eval`, `globalThis`, `Function('return this')()`, `({}).constructor.constructor`, a host function's constructor, an async function's and a generator's). The plan named four roads; a host function's constructor is the one that matters most, since a host function is the only object in the VM the host made. (3) **The global's names** are compared with the language's list plus the five, as in the unit test, and the one symbol QuickJS puts there itself (`Symbol.toStringTag`) is named. (4) **Network:** ten roads are called with a variable's value in the address and each must throw a `ReferenceError` or `TypeError`; a dynamic `import()` of an `https:` and of a `data:` address must be refused. The test listens on the browser context, which sees a worker's requests too, routes a canary host so that an escape would be counted and not lost in a DNS error, and asserts that the only request outside the app's origin is the user's own. `pm.sendRequest` does not exist yet, so there is no exception to make; P3.3 adds exactly the brokered calls. (5) **A forged result:** `postMessage`, `self` and `onmessage` do not exist, and no forged row appears. (6) **State:** a script that changes `Object.prototype`, `JSON`, `Array.prototype.map` and `String` runs three times and finds them whole each time; the page's own are untouched. (7) **Found by the suite:** `setTimeout` took a string and failed only when the timer fired ("not a function"), ending the script. Nothing was evaluated, but a refusal belongs at the call: it now throws a `TypeError` at once. Not given an F-number: no capability was reachable. (8) **The suite was shown to fail:** with `fetch` planted on the VM's global, nine rows fail (`found: fetch` by each road, `extra: fetch`). (9) `docs/scripts.md` describes the suite under the claim ID and the Trust Center's section is rewritten; the limitation bullet for #59 is gone. Closes #59 (F02). **P3.6:** as specified, with one finding. (1) **F66 (#220): the memory limit did not hold.** The first run of this task's own e2e test, a pre-request script that takes 25 typed arrays of 8 MB, ended without an error in three engines. Cause: QuickJS built for WebAssembly counts 8 bytes for an allocation whatever its size (its `malloc_usable_size` returns 0 under Emscripten), so `setMemoryLimit` refuses a single allocation above the limit and nothing else. Measured in Node under the 64 MB limit: 1,840 MB of typed arrays held before the first error, the engine's memory at its 2 GB maximum. The unit test of P3.1 had not seen it because it asked for one allocation of 200 MB, and for an endless loop that stopped at 2 GB with the same message. This contradicts the plan's design (`setMemoryLimit(64 MB)` as the limit) and the docs P3.1 wrote; it is not an escape, and the design closes it, so the session went on and says so in its report. **Fix:** the engine is loaded with a `WebAssembly.Memory` whose maximum is the limit (`wasmMemory` in `newVariant`; `scriptMemory()` in core gives the size, the worker makes the object). Growth past it fails in the browser, the engine's allocator returns nothing, QuickJS throws "out of memory". Measured with the cap: typed arrays, megabyte strings, arrays of numbers and small objects without end each stop within 150 ms with the memory at 61 MB, and the engine runs the next script. With no memory left QuickJS cannot make its error object and throws `null`; `runScript` reports that as the memory limit too. The limit is now on the whole engine, its own few hundred kilobytes included. Four unit tests (`F66: stops a script that allocates 200 MB in pieces…`) were shown failing against the uncapped engine, and the e2e against the built app. `setMemoryLimit` is kept: it refuses a single huge allocation before the memory is asked to grow. (2) **The three limits in the escape suite** (`limits`, three tests in three engines): the pre-request script runs into the limit, the Tests tab shows the message, the script's `catch` did not run, and the post-response script of the same send passes, twice over. The engine is fetched at least three times in the test, which is the worker being ended and started. Messages: "Script timed out after 5000 ms", "Script exceeded memory limit (64 MB)", "Script exceeded the stack limit (too much recursion)". No test asserts a duration; the test's own timeout is 120 s. (3) **The page's own timer** (one second past the deadline, armed when the worker says the engine is loaded) and the recycling were built in P3.1; P3.6 adds their unit tests with a stand-in worker: a run that is never answered ends at deadline plus one second with the timeout message and ends the worker; a result with a limit ends the worker and every run still waiting on it; a message that names no run is ignored. The ten tests of `script-sandbox.spec.ts` are unchanged. (4) Session 3A's numbers are in Appendix A. Budgets are unchanged: the initial bundle moved by 7,885 B raw (0.65%) over the session and is within the limits set in P2.19. Closes #220 (F66). |

The folder is in `.gitignore`. (2) **A library is text, evaluated inside the VM** by the engine's own `eval` (kept by the bootstrap before a script can replace the name). The host hands the text over as a string: no handle crosses, and a library has the script's global and nothing more. The escape suite looks the 35 host names up again with all five loaded, also through Lodash's `get` and `template`. (3) **`require` cannot wait for a download** (the engine is the sync variant, section 5), so the libraries a script names are fetched before it starts: `librariesOf` finds a library by its name written in a script that says `require`. A name put together at run time is refused with a message that says to write it out. Marked in the code with its ceiling. What a script can require does not depend on which scripts ran before: `runScript` is given the libraries of this script only. The worker fetches them before it says "started", so a download is not counted against the script, as with the engine. (4) **The loader is a module of its own**, `@wayfarer/core/library-loader` (a second `paths` entry). Exported from the package's index, every library was emitted twice: the bundler makes a file of each `import()` it meets, in the page's graph too, though nothing there calls it. (5) **What a library looks for in a browser is an argument of its wrapper**, never a global: `self` and `crypto` hold `getRandomValues` backed by the host (crypto-js salts, `uuid.v4`), and `EventTarget` and `Event` are stand-ins that do nothing (chai 6 announces plugins through them). (6) **Versions:** chai 6.3.0 (it bundles its own former dependencies and ships one licence), crypto-js 4.2.0 pinned exactly (R18), lodash 4.18.1, moment 2.31.0 from its `min/moment.min.js`, which has no locales (the main file loads a locale by a computed path, which the bundler turns into every locale), uuid 14.0.3, `@noble/hashes` 2.4.0. All MIT. `npm audit`: 0. (7) **Host-native crypto:** `MD5`, `SHA1`, `SHA256`, `HmacSHA1`, `HmacSHA256` and `enc.Utf8/Hex/Base64`, as the task lists; a WordArray crosses as JSON text (`[sigBytes, word, ...]`), which the engine parses and writes with native code. The host checks the JSON is a list of numbers. (8) **Memory (F66):** all five loaded stay inside the engine's first 16 MB; a script then held 56 MB of text before the limit, against 58 MB with none loaded. Loading all five takes about 75 ms in Node. (9) **Sizes**, each a lazy file: lodash 73,413 B (26,255 B gzip), crypto-js 70,529 B (25,827), chai 66,226 B (17,465), moment 60,949 B (19,691), uuid 10,772 B (4,128). The service worker is unchanged: the files carry a content hash, and the offline test now requires a library. No CSP change, 0 violation events in three engines (R15 did not fire). Nothing else was touched; the claim's sentence says `require` gives five libraries and no other module (C-052, README, Trust Center, ledger). Both are in `e2e/FLAKES.md`. Neither test was changed. **P3.3:** as specified, with these decisions. (1) **The matrix is data, and the suite runs it twice.** `packages/core/test/pm-compat/cases.ts` holds one row per part of Postman's API (102 rows: 66 supported, 20 partial with a note, 16 unsupported), each with a script that proves what the row says; `pm-compat.test.ts` runs them in Node and `src/app/shared/scripts/pm-compat.spec.ts` runs the same rows through `ScriptSandbox` in the real worker in a browser. A change with nowhere to go (no active environment, a request in no collection) is a line in the console. `replaceIn` is core's `VariableResolver` over the scopes as the script has left them, on every one of the objects (a partial row says so). (3) **`pm.request`.** A script is given the request as composed (`scriptRequestOf`: the rows that are switched on; of a multipart form or a file only the mode). A pre-request script's changes come back as JSON text, which the host checks field by field, and `withScriptRequest` puts them into the request that is built; history keeps the request as the user composed it. So the first half of P3.9 (changes apply before resolution) is done here; P3.9 keeps the resolved request for the post-response script, the three durations and the failed pre-request script. (4) **`pm.sendRequest` is brokered twice over:** the engine tells the worker, the worker asks the page, and `RequestExecutor.sendForScript` sends it through `TransportRouter` (so `FetchTransport` or `BridgeTransport`, with `cache: "no-store"`, `credentials: "omit"`, `referrerPolicy: "no-referrer"` and `withDeadline`), under the user's timeout and the send's own cancel signal. Six unit tests carry `@claim:C-007`. **What this is not** (raised by the automated review of the pushed commit, and right): it is not a wall between a script and the vault. D6 already says so (a script plus secret access is an exfiltration path; the control is the review), and P3.9 gives a post-response script the resolved request. While a request is in flight and the script has nothing else to run, the engine's deadline moves by the wait, and the page's watchdog is off until the answer is back. It is in this PR and not one of its own because both touch the register, and each CI round on a PR costs a quarter of an hour. **P3.9:** as specified, with one decision of the maintainer's and four of the session's. **Q6 was asked before the first change and answered yes; asked again when the session found its own description of the check had been wrong** (it had said the comparison "would miss nothing"; it misses a host that a script stored in a variable in an earlier send), **the maintainer chose the one-send prompt with a narrow claim.** As built: (1) **A failed pre-request script stops the send** (maintainer, above). `RequestExecutor.execute` throws `PreRequestScriptError` (a `SendBlockedError`) before the request is built: no vault read, no request, no history entry. It carries the rows of the tests that ran, the failed row, the console and the script's duration; `WorkspaceStore` shows them and opens the Tests tab, and the response panel shows for test rows alone. (2) **The post-response script sees the request as sent, every vault secret masked.** `sentScriptRequest` (core) builds the view from what was built: method, address, headers with the Auth tab's among them, a text body as sent, form fields read back from the sent text, and of a multipart form or a file the mode as before. **The session's decision, not the plan's:** the plan says "the resolved request" and no more; handing a script the plaintext of a vault secret would have been the first place the app does that (a variable's value is the reference, `replaceIn` leaves it, `pm.sendRequest` refuses it), and with `pm.sendRequest` it would have been one line to send it on. The masking is the `Redactor` over the request's secrets, applied to the wire text before the view is made; a credential that is not a vault secret is shown, since a script can read it from the environment anyway. The matrix row `pm.request (post-response)` is therefore "partial", and the legacy `request` row's note says the same (123 rows: 79 supported, 27 partial, 17 unsupported). The maintainer can reverse this in one line of `execute`. What it does not do: a server that sends a secret back hands it to the script in the response, as before. (3) **Three durations.** `RequestExecutionResult.timings` holds `preScriptMs`, `requestMs` and `postScriptMs`; the Tests tab shows them on one line when a script ran ("Pre-request script 12 ms · Request 340 ms · Post-response script 8 ms"). A script's time is the wall time of `ScriptSandbox.execute`, so the first script of a session counts the engine's download and a script counts its `pm.sendRequest` calls; the docs say so. The request's time was already the transport call alone (F11) and is unchanged. (4) **Q6, claim C-053.** `WorkspaceStore.resolveRequest` resolves the host (name and port) of the address as composed with the variables as they were before the pre-request script, and of the address as the script left it with the variables as they are; when they differ and the request refers to a vault secret, `Confirm` asks, naming both, before the vault is read. A no throws `SendDeclinedError`; the executor then sets every variable the script touched back to the value it had (or removes it), in all three scopes. **Without that undo the prompt was a formality:** the script's variables are stored before the request is built, so after "Don't send" a second Send would have found the changed host already in the environment, seen no difference, and sent the secret there unasked. A unit test sends twice and is asked twice. Known edge: a disabled row of the same name that the script's `set` replaced is not brought back. **What the claim does not cover, and says so in the README, the Trust Center and `docs/scripts.md`:** a host that a post-response script, or the pre-request script of a request without a secret, stored in a variable is by the next send the variable's value, and nothing asks. Closing that needs the app to remember which variables a script wrote (a stored shape), which no task has; it is noted for Phase 4 in section 0. A path, a query or a header changed on the same host is not asked about. (5) **Tests changed because the plan changes what they describe:** the unit test of F65 had both scripts fail and asserted "the request is still sent": the failing script there is now the post-response one, and a new test holds the pre-request case to the opposite. The e2e test of F65 swapped its two scripts for the same reason. **The three `@claim:C-052` limit tests** met their limit in the pre-request script and proved "the next script runs" with the post-response script of the same send; that send no longer happens. The limit is now met by the post-response script and the next script is the following send's pre-request script, over three sends instead of two, so that the engine is still loaded three times and the assertion on that count is unchanged; the request count is 3 for 2. The P3.3 unit test that said the post-response script is given the script-changed template is given the built request. No assertion was removed. (6) New tests: 1 in core, 6 in the executor's spec, 8 in `composer-scripts.spec.ts` (two of them `@claim:C-053`), 2 e2e for the ACs in `scripts.spec.ts` (the header reaches `/echo`; the script's 200 ms is in its number and the request's number is smaller than the script's, a relation and not a bound, R6), 1 e2e claim test in `script-redirect.spec.ts`. The 200 ms itself is asserted exactly in the unit test, with the clock stubbed. `protectVariable` moved to `e2e/support/app.ts` for the two specs that share it. (7) Failure first: `PreRequestScriptError` and `timings` did not exist (compile errors), then 5 of 8 composer tests. **P3.12:** as specified. (1) `RequestExecutor.applyChanges` is the one place a script's variable changes are stored; it now hands each scope's changes to `intoVault` first. A `set` on a variable whose stored value is one `{{$secret.<id>}}` reference and nothing else (`protectedSecretId`) is encrypted into that secret with the new `SecretsVault.replaceSecret` (the ciphertext is made before the transaction; `SecretsRepository.replaceCipher` replaces the envelope of a secret that is there and keeps its id and name) and is left out of the changes handed to `changeEnvironment`, `changeGlobals` and `changeCollectionVariables` (P2.10). The variable keeps its reference. (2) **Locked vault:** refused, with `[warn] pm.environment.set("token"): this variable is protected and the vault is locked, so its value was not changed.` The user is not asked for the passphrase in the middle of a script. The request that follows a pre-request script still asks for it when it uses a secret, as before. (3) **Beyond the task's words:** the same in `pm.globals` and `pm.collectionVariables` (a reference can be typed into either); a script that writes back the reference it read changes nothing (without this, a script that copies its variables would have encrypted the text of the reference over the secret); a reference to a secret the vault no longer has is refused with a line of its own; `unset` removes the variable and leaves the secret. (4) **A post-response script's value for a protected variable is not masked** on its way into the vault: the vault is where a secret belongs. Every other value is masked as before. (5) **Known and left:** "protected" is read from this tab's copy of the rows, so a variable another tab protected in the same moment is written as text (marked in the code with what would close it); and when the user answers "Don't send" to C-053's question, a secret the script had already replaced stays replaced, since the old plaintext is gone. (6) Tests: `script-secrets.integration.spec.ts` runs the executor over the real IndexedDB and the real vault: unlocked from a pre-request and from a post-response script, locked, the reference written back, a deleted secret, `unset`; it reads every record of all eight stores and finds neither the new nor the old plaintext. An e2e `@claim:C-007` test in `secrets-wire.spec.ts` has a pre-request script set a protected variable: the new value is in the request the server saw, the console shows it masked, and the scan of all stores, `localStorage` and the console finds it in no encoding. Failure first: 4 of the 6 unit tests ("expected 'old-plaintext-3f9a1c' to be 'new-plaintext-7b2e4d'"). The three `set` rows of the matrix say what the app does with a protected variable. No change to `vault-crypto.ts`, so no mutation run. **P3.10:** measured first, proposed, and decided by the maintainer, as asked. (1) **The benchmark** is `packages/core/bench/script.bench.ts`, run by `npm -w packages/core run bench` (a Vitest config of its own, so it is not part of `npm test` and not in the coverage). It is the task's script: an HMAC-SHA256 over a 1 KB text with crypto-js, the JSON of a 1 MB response, 50 `pm.test` assertions with chai. Each run is a whole `runScript`: a new runtime, the bootstrap, and both libraries evaluated again, which is what a send pays. It asserts that the 50 tests ran and passed, so a script that did no work cannot record a good time. (2) **Numbers:** Appendix A. On a laptop about 38 ms in Node and 36 to 43 ms in the three browsers; on GitHub's runner 64 to 88 ms. R3's limit is 300 ms: the risk did not come true, because the hashes and the response's JSON are done by native code (P3.5, and QuickJS's own `JSON.parse`). (3) **The 25% rule cannot hold, measured:** five jobs of the same commit gave p95 64.3 to 88.4 ms, a spread of 37%. A second measure was tried and dropped: the script's time divided by the time the host's own `JSON.parse` takes for the same body in the same process, which should cancel a slow machine; it ran from 18 to 24 over the same five jobs, 33%, no steadier than the time itself. So was a count of the engine's interrupt callbacks, which is the same on every machine but comes to 8 for this script: too coarse, and blind to the native parts R3 is about. (4) **The gate (maintainer, of three options: this, a 200 ms ceiling, or report only):** the Core job fails when p95 is over 300 ms or the engine's load over 250 ms, which is 3.4 times the slowest reading. It catches a regression of the kind R3 names and not drift; drift is seen in Appendix A, where each session's row records the numbers again. **The AC's "CI fails if p95 regresses more than 25%" is replaced by this**, and the Phase 3 exit criterion "the benchmark gate passes" means this gate. (5) **Where it runs:** in Node, in the Core job, not on "the Chromium runner" of the task's text: V8 runs the same WebAssembly in both, the Core job has no browser to start, and the three browsers measured by hand agree with Node within a few milliseconds. The plan's "WASM cold load" in a browser includes a download, which a CI gate would measure as the runner's network; the one-off readings are in Appendix A. (6) The measurement was taken in the draft of this PR, with a CI step that only printed; the gate was added after the answer. **P3.11:** as specified. (1) `scripts/gen-compat-doc.mjs` (`npm run gen:compat`) writes `docs/postman-compatibility.md` from `CASES`: 123 rows (79 supported, 27 partial, 17 not supported) in 19 sections, one per object of `pm`, one for the older sandbox, one for `console`, timers and encoding, one for `require`. It imports `cases.ts` as it is: Node 24 runs a TypeScript file that needs no compiler, and that file holds data and one interface. **"Generated from the conformance suite" means from its rows, not from a run:** a row is in the document because it is in the file both test runs iterate (`pm-compat.test.ts` in Node, `pm-compat.spec.ts` in the worker), and a row that does not hold fails those. (2) **CI fails on a diff twice over:** `npm run check:compat` in the lint job beside `check:csp`, and a test in `test:scripts` that compares the committed file with what the rows give. (3) **Anchors.** The error messages have linked to `docs/postman-compatibility.md#pm-cookies` and seven other sections since P3.3 and P3.5, and no such file existed. GitHub's anchor for a heading "`pm.cookies`" would be `pmcookies`, so each section carries an explicit `<a id>`: `pm-` and the object's name in lower case, `require`, `legacy`, `globals`. A test reads the anchors out of `vm-bootstrap.ts` (the `unsupported(…, "anchor")` calls and the names given to `refusing`) and every written-out link in the engine and the executor, requires the eight known today to be among them, and requires a section for each. (4) **C-040 reworded**, with its ledger row, the README bullet and its tests: from "a small `pm.*` subset" to "the parts of Postman's script API that `docs/postman-compatibility.md` lists", the document being written from the matrix. The marker is in the README and in the generated document. Its tests are the two it had (Monaco loads; a script runs in the sandbox), every row of the matrix in Node and in the worker (the `it.each` titles carry the tag), and the two tests of the document. `scripts/check-claims.mjs` could not read the title of `it.each(table)("title")`; it can now, with a test. (5) The README's limitation bullet for #77 is gone; the #78 half of that line (no import) stays for Phase 4. (6) Failure first: the document did not exist (`ENOENT`), then the anchor test named a section "-" that was the test's own mistake (its pattern read the `"-"` of a `replace` call as an anchor). **After P3.11, before the release: the two things P3.12 had left, closed** (maintainer: "handle items found along the way"). (1) **A script's value never replaces a stored reference.** `VariableChange` has an optional `keepSecret`; `applyVariableChanges` leaves out a set so marked when a row of that name holds one vault secret's reference, and it runs inside the store's own transaction on the stored rows. `RequestExecutor.applyChanges` marks every change a script makes. So the tab's copy of the rows only decides whether a value is encrypted into the vault; when that copy is a moment old the value is dropped, not stored as text. The editor's changes are not marked: unprotecting a variable is saving its text. Six expectations in `request-executor.spec.ts` name the mark on the changes they assert. (2) **"Don't send" puts a replaced secret back.** `intoVault` reads the secret's plaintext before it replaces it and keeps it, in memory, for that send; the undo of C-053's question writes it back after the variables. Failure first, both in `script-secrets.integration.spec.ts`: "expected 'new-plaintext-7b2e4d' to be 'old-plaintext-3f9a1c'", and the script's text found where the reference had been. Still as it was: a disabled row that a script's `set` replaced is not brought back by the undo. **P3.13:** the release. `package.json` 2.1.0; the changelog's Unreleased section becomes 2.1.0 with a summary; section 0 rewritten for the end of Phase 3 (what Phase 4 must not assume away, and four things left open and written down); P3.13 ticked, both sets of exit criteria marked met with their evidence; Appendix A has the "After session 3B" row; M3 is done. **Budgets reset, because the baseline moved:** the initial bundle is 1,225,230 B raw and 310,823 B gzip, 15,433 B raw over the measurement after 2D that the budgets were set from; they are now that measurement times 1.05 and 1.10 (`angular.json` 1286kb and 1347kb, `bundle:report` 341,900 B gzip), with a line in the changelog. **Section 12:** every finding listed against Phase 3 (F01, F02, F20, F41, F65, F66, F68, and F11's script-time half) is Closed; F64 and F67 are open and belong to no Phase 3 task. **Found in the read before the release:** the README's status note still said "Scripts are still disabled while their sandbox is rebuilt", beside the bullet (C-006) that says they run. It had been false since P3.1 and no check reads that note; it is rewritten for 2.1.0. `docs/scripts.md`, the Trust Center's script section and the 52 rows of `docs/claims.md` were read against the code and needed nothing more. **The Phase 3 exit criterion "scripts run in production" is met in the production build under the production headers, not on the production site,** which still serves a build from before Phase 1; that is said where the criterion is marked. The tag `v2.1.0` is made on the merge commit once the release PR's CI is green (maintainer). **Session 3B in numbers:** 9 PRs (#224, #226, #227, #229, #230, #231, #232, #233 and the release), in two sittings; the maintainer was asked three times in the second (Q6, Q6 again with a correction, the benchmark gate). An address that starts with `-` goes to curl as `--url`. HTTPie reads method, address and items by position: one that starts with `-` writes no command. in both, Chromium's own trace (`ServiceWorkerUpdateChecker::Start`) shows the check for a new worker 2.0 s and 3.06 s after the navigation, and everything after it (the fetch, install, activate, unregister, reload) takes the same 1.05 s. **Ruled out, each by a measurement:** the time between the first load and the navigation (0 to 3 s: no change); the worker's own events (the same fetch events in both, none between 0.55 s and the check); the page's requests (the same list; a page of ten lines with the same worker has its check at 2.0 s whether it asks the worker for something at 0.4, 0.9 or 1.1 s or not); long tasks (none over 50 ms in either build, though on the ten-line page one task of 500 ms or more does move the check by a whole second); `v2.2.0`'s start held back by 40, 80 and 150 ms (the time grows by as much, not by a second). **Found:** with `wayfarer:theme` set to `dark`, `main` takes 3.10 s; with it set to `light`, `v2.2.0` takes 4.04 to 4.17 s; with `index.html` edited to say `data-theme="light"`, `main` takes 3.11 s in a light system and 3.85 s with `dark` stored. So the second comes whenever the theme in use differs from the one `index.html` names, which makes every element's colours change over 0.2 s as the app starts; #268 changed only which theme a headless Chromium ends in (the system's, light, where it was dark). | 2026-10-12 | v1.2.33 | **F84 (#269) fixed (session 5A).** **The failing test first:** `e2e/service-worker.spec.ts` › "@claim:C-015 offline after one visit, every script that visit loaded is there, the lazy ones too: the editor opens (F84)". On the build of `main` at `ea8df75`, in Chromium, the worker's cache lacked 14 of the scripts the visit had loaded (`/chunk-C6MKPLDi.js` first) after the test's 5 s. **The fix, two parts.** (1) `HASHED` in `src/sw.ts` takes a hash of either case with `_` and `-`: the build's lazy chunks have one (`chunk-iAuoh2-d.js`). (2) A first visit loads its first lazy chunks before the worker controls the page: when the worker takes control, `SwUpdate` sends it the names of what the page has loaded (`performance.getEntriesByType("resource")`), and the worker keeps those that are same-origin and hashed, as its fetch handler would have. A later visit loads everything through the worker and sends nothing. **What the test asserts, and what it does not.** That every script the first visit loaded is in the cache; that with the server stopped the editor mounts again; that no request fails for a file that visit had loaded. A file the visit had not asked for yet (WebKit fetched a language's worker and two of its chunks only later) fails offline and is not asserted: nothing precaches the 12.9 MB of lazy files, and `docs/trust-center.md`'s Known limitations now says what offline holds. The first form of the test asked for no failed request at all and failed in WebKit for that reason; and it waited 5 s for an editor that Firefox mounts 5.06 s into a first visit, on `main`'s build as on the branch's (measured), so that wait is 15 s. **C-015's sentence is unchanged.** **Measured:** 15 of 15 in the three claims projects, five repeats each. **The full local run before the PR:** lint, core typecheck, core tests 864 (lines 99.57%), bench, `test:ci` 639 (lines 79.14%), build, `bundle:report` (initial 321.6 kB gzip, under 358,200 B), knip, `check:claims` (61), `check:csp`, `check:compat`, `test:scripts`, `bridge:test`, `npm audit` 0; e2e in three engines with retries 0: 867 tests, 859 passed, 8 skipped by design, 0 failed. A cause in the fix: the page listed what it had loaded at the moment the worker took control, and on the runner those chunks were still on their way then, asked for before the worker and so not through it. The page now watches (`PerformanceObserver`, with what is already there) and sends every file that was asked for before that moment, when it arrives. 90 of 90 in three repeats of the spec in three engines, and the full local run again, every step with exit 0: e2e 867 tests, 859 passed, 8 skipped, 0 failed. ## Appendix B — Audit evidence (2026-09-27, production)

- Prod sandbox worker `worker-SEEDX3S5.js` served with `content-security-policy: … script-src 'self' …`. Posting a run message returned `error: "Evaluating a string as JavaScript violates the following Content Security Policy directive because 'unsafe-eval' is not an allowed source of script"`.
- `GET https://httpbin.org/html` rendered `200 OK` with body `{"error": {}, "text": "<!DOCTYPE html>…"}`. `/image/png` rendered binary as text.
- A request to an unresolvable host rendered `504 Gateway Timeout` in about 20 ms.
- Page console on load: Cloudflare Insights beacon blocked by CSP; an inline Cloudflare challenge script blocked by CSP.
- The accessibility snapshot showed buttons named `bolt`, `upload` and `light_mode`.
